<p class="mw-empty-elt">
</p>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Coat of arms</th><td class="infobox-data"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:C_o_a_popes_Della_Rovere.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="Julius II&#39;s coat of arms"><img alt="Julius II&#39;s coat of arms" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/C_o_a_popes_Della_Rovere.svg/50px-C_o_a_popes_Della_Rovere.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="60" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/C_o_a_popes_Della_Rovere.svg/75px-C_o_a_popes_Della_Rovere.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/C_o_a_popes_Della_Rovere.svg/100px-C_o_a_popes_Della_Rovere.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1031" data-file-height="1238" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below"><a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Pope Julius (disambiguation)">Other popes  named Julius</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope Julius II</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Iulius II</i>; <a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Giulio II</i>; born <b>Giuliano della Rovere</b>; 5 December 1443&#160;&#8211;&#32;21 February 1513) was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> from 1503 to his death, in February 1513. Nicknamed the <b>Warrior Pope</b> or the <b>Fearsome Pope</b>, he chose his <a href="/wiki/Papal_name" title="Papal name">papal name</a> not in honour of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_I" title="Pope Julius I">Pope Julius I</a> but in emulation of <a href="/wiki/Julius_Caesar" title="Julius Caesar">Julius Caesar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> One of the most powerful and influential popes, Julius II was a central figure of the <a href="/wiki/High_Renaissance" title="High Renaissance">High Renaissance</a> and left a significant cultural and political legacy.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> As a result of his policies during the <a href="/wiki/Italian_Wars" title="Italian Wars">Italian Wars</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> increased its power and centralization, and the office of the papacy continued to be crucial, diplomatically and politically, during the entirety of the 16th century in Italy and Europe.
</p><p>In 1506, Julius II established the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Museums" title="Vatican Museums">Vatican Museums</a> and initiated the rebuilding of the <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a>. The same year he organized the famous <a href="/wiki/Swiss_Guard" title="Swiss Guard">Swiss Guard</a> for his personal protection and commanded a successful campaign in Romagna against local lords. The interests of Julius II lay also in the <a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a>, as he ratified the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Tordesillas" title="Treaty of Tordesillas">Treaty of Tordesillas</a>, establishing the <a href="/wiki/First_bishoprics_in_the_Americas" class="mw-redirect" title="First bishoprics in the Americas">first bishoprics in the Americas</a> and beginning the <a href="/wiki/Catholicization" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholicization">Catholicization</a> of Latin America. In 1508, he commissioned the <a href="/wiki/Raphael_Rooms" title="Raphael Rooms">Raphael Rooms</a> and <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>'s paintings in the <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Chapel" title="Sistine Chapel">Sistine Chapel</a>.
</p><p>Julius II was described by <a href="/wiki/Machiavelli" class="mw-redirect" title="Machiavelli">Machiavelli</a> in his works as an ideal prince. Pope Julius II allowed people seeking <a href="/wiki/Indulgence" title="Indulgence">indulgences</a> to donate money to the Church, which would be used for the construction of Saint Peter's Basilica.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> He was fiercely satirized after his death by <a href="/wiki/Erasmus_of_Rotterdam" class="mw-redirect" title="Erasmus of Rotterdam">Erasmus of Rotterdam</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/Julius_Excluded_from_Heaven" title="Julius Excluded from Heaven">Julius Excluded from Heaven</a></i>, in which the drunken pope, denied entry by St Peter, justifies his worldly life and threatens to found his own paradise.<sup id="cite_ref-Britannica_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britannica-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Overview_of_the_Italian_politics_of_his_reign">Overview of the Italian politics of his reign</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Overview of the Italian politics of his reign">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Scuola_veneta,_busto_di_papa_giulio_II_della_rovere,_1503-13_ca.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Scuola_veneta%2C_busto_di_papa_giulio_II_della_rovere%2C_1503-13_ca.jpg/220px-Scuola_veneta%2C_busto_di_papa_giulio_II_della_rovere%2C_1503-13_ca.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="366" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Scuola_veneta%2C_busto_di_papa_giulio_II_della_rovere%2C_1503-13_ca.jpg/330px-Scuola_veneta%2C_busto_di_papa_giulio_II_della_rovere%2C_1503-13_ca.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Scuola_veneta%2C_busto_di_papa_giulio_II_della_rovere%2C_1503-13_ca.jpg/440px-Scuola_veneta%2C_busto_di_papa_giulio_II_della_rovere%2C_1503-13_ca.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2237" data-file-height="3719" /></a><figcaption>Bust of Julius II</figcaption></figure>
<p>Julius II became pope in the context of the <a href="/wiki/Italian_Wars" title="Italian Wars">Italian Wars</a>, a period in which the major powers of Europe fought for primacy in the Italian peninsula. <a href="/wiki/Louis_XII_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XII of France">Louis XII of France</a> controlled the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Milan" title="Duchy of Milan">Duchy of Milan</a>, previously held by the <a href="/wiki/Sforza" class="mw-redirect" title="Sforza">Sforzas</a>, and French influence had replaced that of the <a href="/wiki/Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Medici">Medici</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Florence" title="Republic of Florence">Republic of Florence</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Naples" title="Kingdom of Naples">Kingdom of Naples</a> was under Spanish rule, and the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Borgia" title="House of Borgia">Borja</a> family from Spain was a major political faction in the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> following the reign of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_VI" title="Pope Alexander VI">Alexander VI</a>. Archduke <a href="/wiki/Maximilian_I_of_Austria" class="mw-redirect" title="Maximilian I of Austria">Maximilian I of Austria</a> was hostile to France and Venice, and desired to descend into Italy in order to be crowned <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_emperor" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Roman emperor">Holy Roman emperor</a> by the pope. The <a href="/wiki/Conclave_capitulation" title="Conclave capitulation">conclave capitulation</a> preceding his election included several terms, such as the opening of an <a href="/wiki/Ecumenical_council" title="Ecumenical council">ecumenical council</a> and the organization of a crusade against the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Turks" title="Ottoman Turks">Ottoman Turks</a>. Once crowned, Julius II proclaimed instead his goal to centralize the Papal States (in large part a patchwork of communes and <i>signorie</i>) and "free Italy from the barbarians".<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his early years as pope, Julius II removed the Borjas from power and exiled them to Spain. <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare Borgia</a>, Duke of Romagna, shared the same fate and lost his possessions.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Burgkmair_Julius_II.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Burgkmair_Julius_II.jpg/220px-Burgkmair_Julius_II.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="221" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Burgkmair_Julius_II.jpg/330px-Burgkmair_Julius_II.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Burgkmair_Julius_II.jpg/440px-Burgkmair_Julius_II.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1328" data-file-height="1334" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Woodcut" title="Woodcut">Woodcut</a> by <a href="/wiki/Hans_Burgkmair" title="Hans Burgkmair">Hans Burgkmair</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>He joined an anti-Venetian <a href="/wiki/League_of_Cambrai" class="mw-redirect" title="League of Cambrai">league formed in Cambrai</a> between France, Spain, and Austria, with the goal of capturing the coast of <a href="/wiki/Romagna" title="Romagna">Romagna</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Venetian_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Venetian Republic">Venetian Republic</a>. Having achieved this goal, he formed an anti-French "Holy League" with Venice following the defeat of the latter at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Agnadello" title="Battle of Agnadello">Battle of Agnadello</a>. His main goal was now again to "expel the barbarians" (<i>Fuori i Barbari!</i>). Julius II brought the Catholic <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon" title="Ferdinand II of Aragon">Ferdinand II of Aragon</a> into the alliance, declaring Naples a papal fief and promising a formal investiture.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
Having previously declared that the Imperial election was sufficient for Maximilian to style himself as Holy Roman Emperor, he later obtained Habsburg support against France as well. Julius II personally led the Papal armed forces at the victorious <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Mirandola_(1511)" title="Siege of Mirandola (1511)">Siege of Mirandola</a> and, despite subsequent defeats and great losses at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Ravenna_(1512)" title="Battle of Ravenna (1512)">Battle of Ravenna</a>, he ultimately forced the French troops of Louis XII to retreat behind the Alps after the arrival of <a href="/wiki/Swiss_mercenaries" title="Swiss mercenaries">Swiss mercenaries</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Holy Roman Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the Congress of <a href="/wiki/Mantua" title="Mantua">Mantua</a> in 1512, Julius II ordered the restoration of Italian families to power in the vacuum of French rule: the Imperial Swiss led by <a href="/wiki/Massimiliano_Sforza" class="mw-redirect" title="Massimiliano Sforza">Massimiliano Sforza</a> restored Sforza rule in Milan, and a Spanish army led by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_X" title="Pope Leo X">Giovanni de Medici</a> restored Medici rule in Florence. The Kingdom of Naples was recognized as a <a href="/wiki/Papal_fief" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal fief">papal fief</a>. The Venetians regained their territories lost to France, and the Papal States annexed <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Parma_and_Piacenza" title="Duchy of Parma and Piacenza">Parma</a> and <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Modena_and_Reggio" title="Duchy of Modena and Reggio">Modena</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Conciliarism" title="Conciliarism">conciliarist</a> movement promoted by foreign monarchs was crushed, and Julius II affirmed <a href="/wiki/Ultramontanism" title="Ultramontanism">ultramontanism</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Fifth_Lateran_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="Fifth Lateran Council">Fifth Lateran Council</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> This is often presented in traditional historiography as the moment in which Renaissance Italy came the closest to unification after the end of the <a href="/wiki/Italic_League" title="Italic League">Italic League</a> of the 15th century. However, Julius II was far away from the possibility to form a single Italian kingdom, if that was his goal at all, since foreign armies were largely involved in his wars and the French were preparing new campaigns against the Swiss for Milan. Naples, even if recognized as a papal fief, was still under Spain and in fact Julius II was planning to end Spanish presence in the south.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, by the end of his pontificate, the papal objective to make the Church the main force in the Italian Wars was achieved. At the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Carnival" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Carnival">Roman Carnival</a> of 1513, Julius II presented himself as the "liberator of Italy".<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julius planned to call for a crusade against the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a> in order to retake <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a>, but died before making official announcements.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> His successor, Pope Leo X, along with Emperor Maximilian, would re-establish the <a href="/wiki/Status_quo_ante_bellum" title="Status quo ante bellum">status quo ante bellum</a> in Italy by ratifying the treaties of Brussels and Noyon in 1516; France regained control of Milan after the victory of <a href="/wiki/Francis_I_of_France" title="Francis I of France">Francis I</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Marignano" title="Battle of Marignano">Battle of Marignano</a>, and Spain was recognized as the direct ruler of Naples.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Giuliano della Rovere <a href="/wiki/Albisola" class="mw-redirect" title="Albisola">Albisola</a> was born near <a href="/wiki/Savona" title="Savona">Savona</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Genoa" title="Genoa">Republic of Genoa</a>. He was of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_della_Rovere" class="mw-redirect" title="House of della Rovere">House of della Rovere</a>, a noble but impoverished family, the son of <a href="/w/index.php?title=Raffaello_della_Rovere&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Raffaello della Rovere (page does not exist)">Raffaello della Rovere</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raffaello_della_Rovere" class="extiw" title="it:Raffaello della Rovere">it</a>&#93;</span><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> and Theodora Manerola, a woman of <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greek</a> ancestry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEShaw199710_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShaw199710-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He had three brothers: Bartolomeo, a Franciscan friar who then became <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Ferrara-Comacchio" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Ferrara-Comacchio">Bishop of Ferrara</a> (1474–1494);<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel1914&#91;httpsarchiveorgdetailshierarchiacathol02eubeuoftpage153_p._153&#93;_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel1914[httpsarchiveorgdetailshierarchiacathol02eubeuoftpage153_p._153]-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Leonardo; and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_della_Rovere" class="mw-redirect" title="Giovanni della Rovere">Giovanni</a>, Prefect of the City of Rome (1475–1501)<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> and Prince of Sora and Senigallia. He also had a sister, Lucina (later the mother of Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Sisto_Gara_della_Rovere" title="Sisto Gara della Rovere">Sisto Gara della Rovere</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPlatina1568364_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlatina1568364-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Giuliano was educated by his uncle, Fr. Francesco della Rovere, <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor" title="Order of Friars Minor">O.F.M.</a>, among the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscans</a>, who took him under his special charge. He was later sent by this same uncle (who by that time had become <a href="/wiki/List_of_Ministers_General_of_the_Order_of_Friars_Minor" title="List of Ministers General of the Order of Friars Minor">Minister General of the Franciscans</a> (1464–1469)), to the Franciscan friary in <a href="/wiki/Perugia" title="Perugia">Perugia</a>, where he could study the sciences at the University.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDumesnil1873p._6:_&quot;&#39;&#39;Lorsqu&#39;_il_fut_devenu_gḗnḗral_de_cet_ordre,_Sixte_l&#39;attacha_au_Couvent_de_Pḗrouse,_afin_qu&#39;il_y_apprît_les_sciences&#39;&#39;&quot;_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDumesnil1873p._6:_&quot;&#39;&#39;Lorsqu&#39;_il_fut_devenu_gḗnḗral_de_cet_ordre,_Sixte_l&#39;attacha_au_Couvent_de_Pḗrouse,_afin_qu&#39;il_y_apprît_les_sciences&#39;&#39;&quot;-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Della Rovere, as a young man, showed traits of being rough, coarse and given to bad language. During the late 1490s, he became more closely acquainted with Cardinal de’ Medici and his cousin <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_VII" title="Pope Clement VII">Giulio de’ Medici</a>, both of whom would later become Pope, (i.e. Leo X and Clement VII, respectively). The two dynasties became uneasy allies in the context of papal politics. Both houses desired an end to the occupation of Italian lands by the armies of France. He seemed less enthused by theology; rather, <a href="/wiki/Paul_Strathern" title="Paul Strathern">Paul Strathern</a> argues, his imagined heroes were military leaders such as <a href="/w/index.php?title=Frederic_Colonna&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Frederic Colonna (page does not exist)">Frederic Colonna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrathern2003246–248_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrathern2003246–248-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cardinalate">Cardinalate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Cardinalate">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JuliuIIcoin.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/12/JuliuIIcoin.jpg/220px-JuliuIIcoin.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="110" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/12/JuliuIIcoin.jpg/330px-JuliuIIcoin.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/12/JuliuIIcoin.jpg/440px-JuliuIIcoin.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="300" /></a><figcaption>Giuliano della Rovere (left, future Julius II), and Julius II's nephew, <a href="/wiki/Clemente_Grosso_della_Rovere" title="Clemente Grosso della Rovere">Clemente della Rovere</a> (right), who safeguarded Giuliano's affairs while he fled to France following a dispute with Alexander VI</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Melozzo_da_forl%C3%AC,_Sisto_IV_nomina_Bartolomeo_Platina_Prefetto_della_Biblioteca_Vaticana,_1477_ca._02_(cropped2).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Melozzo_da_forl%C3%AC%2C_Sisto_IV_nomina_Bartolomeo_Platina_Prefetto_della_Biblioteca_Vaticana%2C_1477_ca._02_%28cropped2%29.JPG/220px-Melozzo_da_forl%C3%AC%2C_Sisto_IV_nomina_Bartolomeo_Platina_Prefetto_della_Biblioteca_Vaticana%2C_1477_ca._02_%28cropped2%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="176" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Melozzo_da_forl%C3%AC%2C_Sisto_IV_nomina_Bartolomeo_Platina_Prefetto_della_Biblioteca_Vaticana%2C_1477_ca._02_%28cropped2%29.JPG/330px-Melozzo_da_forl%C3%AC%2C_Sisto_IV_nomina_Bartolomeo_Platina_Prefetto_della_Biblioteca_Vaticana%2C_1477_ca._02_%28cropped2%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/Melozzo_da_forl%C3%AC%2C_Sisto_IV_nomina_Bartolomeo_Platina_Prefetto_della_Biblioteca_Vaticana%2C_1477_ca._02_%28cropped2%29.JPG/440px-Melozzo_da_forl%C3%AC%2C_Sisto_IV_nomina_Bartolomeo_Platina_Prefetto_della_Biblioteca_Vaticana%2C_1477_ca._02_%28cropped2%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1626" data-file-height="1300" /></a><figcaption>Giulliano della Rovere, as cardinal (left), with his uncle and patron Francesco della Rovere, Pope Sixtus IV (right)</figcaption></figure>
<p>After his uncle was elected <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_IV" title="Pope Sixtus IV">Pope Sixtus IV</a> on 10 August 1471,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel191415_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel191415-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Giuliano was appointed <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Diocese_of_Carpentras" title="Ancient Diocese of Carpentras">Bishop of Carpentras</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Comtat_Venaissin" title="Comtat Venaissin">Comtat Venaissin</a> on 16 October 1471.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel1914119_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel1914119-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> In an act of overt <a href="/wiki/Nepotism" title="Nepotism">nepotism</a> he was immediately raised to the <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholicism)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal (Catholicism)">cardinalate</a> on 16 December 1471, and assigned the same <a href="/wiki/Titular_church" title="Titular church">titular church</a> as that formerly held by his uncle, <a href="/wiki/San_Pietro_in_Vincoli" title="San Pietro in Vincoli">San Pietro in Vincoli</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel191416_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel191416-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Guilty of serial <a href="/wiki/Simony" title="Simony">simony</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pluralities" class="mw-redirect" title="Pluralities">pluralism</a>, he held several powerful offices at once: in addition to the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Avignon" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Avignon">archbishopric of Avignon</a> he held no fewer than eight bishoprics, including <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Lausanne" title="Bishop of Lausanne">Lausanne</a> from 1472, and <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Coutances_and_Avranches" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Coutances and Avranches">Coutances</a> (1476–1477).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._16_n._2_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._16_n._2-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1474, Giuliano led an army to <a href="/wiki/Todi" title="Todi">Todi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Spoleto" title="Spoleto">Spoleto</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Citt%C3%A0_di_Castello" title="Città di Castello">Città di Castello</a> as papal legate. He returned to <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> in May in the company of Duke <a href="/wiki/Federigo_of_Urbino" class="mw-redirect" title="Federigo of Urbino">Federigo of Urbino</a>, who promised his daughter in marriage to Giuliano's brother Giovanni, who was subsequently named Lord of <a href="/wiki/Senigallia" title="Senigallia">Senigallia</a> and of <a href="/wiki/Mondov%C3%AC" title="Mondovì">Mondovì</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_pp._253–254_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_pp._253–254-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> On 22 December 1475, Pope Sixtus IV created the new Archdiocese of Avignon, assigning to it as suffragan dioceses the Sees of Vaison, Cavaillon, and Carpentras. He appointed Giuliano as the first archbishop. Giuliano held the archdiocese until his later election to the papacy. In 1476 the office of Legate was added, and he left Rome for France in February. On 22 August 1476 he founded the <i>Collegium de Ruvere</i> in <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a>. He returned to Rome on 4 October 1476.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1479, Cardinal Giuliano served his one-year term as Chamberlain of the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">College of Cardinals</a>. In this office he was responsible for collecting all the revenues owed to the cardinals as a group (from <i><a href="/wiki/Quinquennial_visit_ad_limina" title="Quinquennial visit ad limina">ad limina</a></i> visits, for example) and for the proper disbursements of appropriate shares to cardinals who were in service in the Roman Curia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel191457–59_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel191457–59-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Giuliano was again named Papal Legate to France on 28 April 1480, and left Rome on 9 June. As Legate, his mission was threefold: to make peace between King <a href="/wiki/Louis_XI" title="Louis XI">Louis XI</a> and the Emperor <a href="/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor">Maximilian of Austria</a>; to raise funds for a war against the Ottoman Turks; and to negotiate the release of Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Jean_Balue#Imprisoned" title="Jean Balue">Jean Balue</a> and Bishop Guillaume d'Harancourt (who by then had been imprisoned by Louis for eleven years on charges of treason). He reached Paris in September, and finally, on 20 December 1480, Louis gave orders that Balue be handed over to the Archpriest of Loudun, who had been commissioned by the Legate to receive him in the name of the Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._43_nn._423,_426_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._43_nn._423,_426-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> He returned to Rome on 3 February 1482.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._44_n._454_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._44_n._454-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Shortly thereafter the sum of 300,000 ecus of gold was received from the French in a subsidy of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDumesnil1873pp._10–11_(with_a_defective_chronology)_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDumesnil1873pp._10–11_(with_a_defective_chronology)-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 31 January 1483 Cardinal della Rovere was promoted suburbicarian <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Suburbicarian_Diocese_of_Ostia" title="Roman Catholic Suburbicarian Diocese of Ostia">Bishop of Ostia</a>, in succession to Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Guillaume_d%27Estouteville" title="Guillaume d&#39;Estouteville">Guillaume d'Estouteville</a> who had died on 22 January.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel191460_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel191460-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> It was the privilege of the Bishop of Ostia to consecrate an elected pope a bishop, if he were not already a bishop. This actually occurred in the case of <a href="/wiki/Pius_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius III">Pius III</a> (Francesco Todeschini-Piccolomini), who was ordained a priest on 30 September 1503 and consecrated a bishop on 1 October 1503 by Cardinal Giuliano della Rovere.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurchard1885III,_pp._280–281_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurchard1885III,_pp._280–281-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Around this time, in 1483, an illegitimate daughter was born, <a href="/wiki/Felice_della_Rovere" title="Felice della Rovere">Felice della Rovere</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMurphy2005pp._xv,_11_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurphy2005pp._xv,_11-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 3 November 1483, Cardinal della Rovere was named <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Bologna" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Bologna">Bishop of Bologna</a> and Papal Legate, succeeding Cardinal Francesco Gonzaga, who had died on 21 October. He held the diocese until 1502.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> On 28 December 1484, Giuliano participated in the investiture of his brother Giovanni as Captain-General of the Papal Armies by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_VIII" title="Pope Innocent VIII">Pope Innocent VIII</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurchard1883&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidMNMDUhGaiDICpgPA124_I,_p._124&#93;_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurchard1883[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidMNMDUhGaiDICpgPA124_I,_p._124]-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1484 Giuliano was living in the new palazzo which he had constructed next to the Basilica of the Twelve Apostles, which he had also restored. Pope Sixtus IV paid a formal visit to the newly restored building on 1 May 1482, and it may be that Giuliano was already in residence then.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="War_with_Naples">War with Naples</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: War with Naples">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p><a href="/wiki/Sixtus_IV" class="mw-redirect" title="Sixtus IV">Sixtus IV</a> died on 12 August 1484 and was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Innocent_VIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Innocent VIII">Innocent VIII</a>. After the ceremonies of the election of Pope Innocent were completed, the cardinals were dismissed to their own homes, but Cardinal della Rovere accompanied the new Pope to the Vatican Palace and was the only one to remain with him. Ludwig Pastor quotes the Florentine ambassador as remarking, "[Pope Innocent] gives the impression of a man who is guided rather by the advice of others than by his own lights." The ambassador of <a href="/wiki/Ferrara" title="Ferrara">Ferrara</a> stated, "While with his uncle [Della Rovere] had not the slightest influence, he now obtains whatever he likes from the new Pope."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902V,_p._242_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902V,_p._242-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Della Rovere was one of the five cardinals named to the committee to make the arrangements for the Coronation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurchard1883I,_p._75_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurchard1883I,_p._75-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1485 Pope Innocent and Cardinal della Rovere (as the Pope's new principal advisor) decided to involve themselves in the political affairs of the Kingdom of Naples, in what was called the <i><a href="/wiki/Conspiracy_of_the_Barons" title="Conspiracy of the Barons">Conspiracy of the Barons</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_pp._291–302_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_pp._291–302-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> On Palm Sunday, 20 March, Cardinal della Rovere, concealing his activities from his principal rival, <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_Rodrigo_Borgia" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal Rodrigo Borgia">Cardinal Rodrigo Borgia</a> (later Pope Alexander VI), rode out of Rome and departed by sea from <a href="/wiki/Ostia_(Rome)" title="Ostia (Rome)">Ostia</a>, intending to head for Genoa and Avignon to prepare to wage war between the Church and the King of Naples, <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Naples" title="Ferdinand I of Naples">Ferdinand I</a> (Ferrante).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurchard1883I,_p._183._A_note_in_the_&#39;&#39;Acta_Cameralia&#39;&#39;_records_that_Della_Rovere_returned_to_Rome_on_12_September_1486:_&#91;&#91;#CITEREFEubel1914&#124;Eubel_1914&#93;&#93;,_p._49_n._521_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurchard1883I,_p._183._A_note_in_the_&#39;&#39;Acta_Cameralia&#39;&#39;_records_that_Della_Rovere_returned_to_Rome_on_12_September_1486:_[[#CITEREFEubel1914|Eubel_1914]],_p._49_n._521-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> On 28 June the Pope sent back to Naples the token gift of a <a href="/wiki/Palfrey" title="Palfrey">palfrey</a> which symbolized the King of Naples' submission and demanded the full feudal submission of the Kingdom of Naples to the Roman Church according to long-standing tradition. In a second attempt to overthrow the Aragonese monarchy, the <a href="/wiki/Prince_of_Salerno" class="mw-redirect" title="Prince of Salerno">Prince of Salerno</a> Antonello II di Sanseverino, on the advice of Antonello Petrucci and Francesco Coppola, gathered together several feudal families belonging to the Guelph faction and supporting the Angevin claim to Naples. Antonello de Sanseverino was the brother-in-law of Cardinal della Rovere's brother Giovanni, who was a noble of Naples because of his fief of Sora. The principal complaints of the barons were the heavy taxation imposed by Ferdinand to finance his war against the Saracens, who had occupied Bari in 1480; and the vigorous efforts of Ferrante to centralize the administrative apparatus of the kingdom, moving it away from a feudal to a bureaucratic system. The barons seized L'Aquila and appealed to the Pope for assistance as their feudal overlord. Genoa and Venice supported the Papacy, while Florence and Milan opted for Naples. In Rome, the Orsini allied themselves with Ferrante's son <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_II_of_Naples" title="Alfonso II of Naples">Alfonso</a>, and therefore the Colonna supported the Pope in the street fighting that ensued.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_p._293_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_p._293-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Ferrante reacted by seizing the fiefs of the barons, and, when the two parties met to negotiate a settlement, Ferrante had them arrested, and eventually executed. The prestige of the della Rovere family was seriously damaged, and in an attempt to exculpate himself Pope Innocent began to withdraw his support for them. Peace was restored in 1487, but Innocent VIII's papacy was discredited.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECreighton1903IV,_pp._140–145_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECreighton1903IV,_pp._140–145-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Papal_ambassador">Papal ambassador</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Papal ambassador">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Raffael_092.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Raffael_092.jpg/220px-Raffael_092.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="269" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Raffael_092.jpg/330px-Raffael_092.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Raffael_092.jpg/440px-Raffael_092.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2024" data-file-height="2473" /></a><figcaption>Julius by <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, in the <i><a href="/wiki/The_Mass_at_Bolsena" title="The Mass at Bolsena">Mass at Bolsena</a></i></figcaption></figure>
<p>On 23 March 1486, the pope sent Giuliano as Papal Legate to the Court of <a href="/wiki/Charles_VIII_of_France" title="Charles VIII of France">King Charles VIII</a> of France to ask for help. A French entourage arrived in Rome on 31 May, but immediately relations broke down with the pro-Spanish Cardinal Rodrigo. But Ferrante's army decided the pope's humiliation, Innocent backed down and on 10 August signed a treaty. Innocent looked for new allies and settled on the Republic of Florence.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>On 2 March 1487, Giuliano was appointed legate in the March of Ancona and to the Republic of Venice. He encouraged trade with the sizable Turkish community at these ports. But urgent reports arrived from the King of Hungary that the Ottoman Sultan was threatening Italy. He returned on 8 April 1488, and again took up his residence in the <a href="/wiki/Palazzo_Colonna" title="Palazzo Colonna">Palazzo Colonna</a> next to the <a href="/wiki/Santi_Apostoli,_Rome" title="Santi Apostoli, Rome">Basilica of the XII Apostles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._49_n._523_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._49_n._523-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Conclave_of_1492">Conclave of 1492</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Conclave of 1492">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the Conclave of 1492, following the death of Innocent VIII, Cardinal della Rovere was supported for election by both King <a href="/wiki/Charles_VIII_of_France" title="Charles VIII of France">Charles VIII of France</a> and by Charles' enemy <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Naples" title="Ferdinand I of Naples">King Ferrante of Naples</a>. It was reported that France had deposited 200,000 ducats into a bank account to promote della Rovere's candidature, while the Republic of Genoa had deposited 100,000 ducats to the same end. Della Rovere, however, had enemies, both because of the influence he had exercised over Pope Sixtus IV and because of his French sympathies. His rivals included Cardinal Ardicio della Porta and Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Ascanio_Sforza" title="Ascanio Sforza">Ascanio Sforza</a>, both patronized by the Milanese.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902V,_pp._378–381_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902V,_pp._378–381-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Kellogg, Baynes &amp; Smith, continue, a "rivalry had, however, gradually grown up between [della Rovere] and [then-Cardinal] <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_VI" title="Pope Alexander VI">Rodrigo Borgia</a>, and on the death of <a href="/wiki/Innocent_VIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Innocent VIII">Innocent VIII</a> in 1492 Borgia by means of a secret agreement and simony with Ascanio Sforza succeeded in being elected by a large majority, under the name of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_VI" title="Pope Alexander VI">Pope Alexander VI</a>."<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Della Rovere, jealous and angry, hated Borgia for being elected over him.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 31 August 1492 the new Pope, Alexander VI, held a consistory in which he named six cardinal legates, one of whom was Giuliano della Rovere, who was appointed Legate in Avignon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._50_n._545_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._50_n._545-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Cardinal Giuliano was increasingly alarmed by the powerful position assumed by Cardinal Ascanio Sforza and the Milanese faction in the Court of Alexander VI, and after Christmas Day in December 1492 chose to withdraw to his fortress in the town and diocese of Ostia, at the mouth of the Tiber River.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> In that same month, Federico of Altamura, the second son of King Ferdinando (Ferrante) of Naples was in Rome to pay homage to the new pope, and he reported back to his father that Alexander and Cardinal Sforza were working on establishing new alliances, which would upset Ferrante's security arrangements. Ferrante, therefore, decided to use Della Rovere as the center of an anti-Sforza party at the papal court, a prospect made easier since Ferrante had prudently repaired his relations with Cardinal Giuliano after the War of the Barons. He also warned King Ferdinand and Queen Isabella of Spain that Alexander was intriguing with the French, which brought an immediate visit of a Spanish ambassador to the Pope. In June Federico of Altamura was back in Rome and held conversations with Della Rovere, assuring him of Neapolitan protection. On 24 July 1493, Cardinal della Rovere returned to Rome (despite the warnings of Virginius Orsini) and dined with the Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_pp._336–340,_346–348_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_pp._336–340,_346–348-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Charles_VIII_and_the_French_war_over_Naples">Charles VIII and the French war over Naples</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Charles VIII and the French war over Naples">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Della Rovere at once determined to take refuge from Borgia's wrath at <a href="/wiki/Ostia_(Rome)" title="Ostia (Rome)">Ostia</a>. On 23 April 1494, the Cardinal took ship, having placed his fortress at Ostia in the hands of his brother Giovanni della Rovere, and traveled to Genoa and then to Avignon. He was summoned by King Charles VIII to <a href="/wiki/Lyon" title="Lyon">Lyons</a>, where the two met on 1 June 1494.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_p._357_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.1,_p._357-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> He joined <a href="/wiki/Charles_VIII_of_France" title="Charles VIII of France">Charles VIII of France</a> who undertook to take Italy back from the Borgias by military force.<sup id="cite_ref-Ott1910_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ott1910-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> The King entered Rome with his army on 31 December 1495, with Giuliano della Rovere riding on one side and Cardinal Ascanio Sforza riding on the other. The King made several demands of Pope Alexander, one of which was that the <a href="/wiki/Castel_Sant%27Angelo" title="Castel Sant&#39;Angelo">Castel Sant'Angelo</a> be turned over to French forces.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> This Pope Alexander refused to do, claiming that Cardinal della Rovere would occupy it and become master of Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Charles soon conquered <a href="/wiki/Naples" title="Naples">Naples</a>, making his triumphal entry on 22 February 1495, but he was forced to remove most of his army. As he was returning to the north, his army was defeated at the Battle of Foronovo on 5 July 1495, and his Italian adventure came to an end. The last remnants of the French invasion were gone by November 1496.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Ostia, however, remained in French hands until March 1497, causing difficulties in the provisioning of the city of Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902V,_p._491_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902V,_p._491-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Back in Lyon in 1496, Charles VIII and Giuliano della Rovere were planning another war. Giuliano was traveling back and forth from Lyon to Avignon, raising troops. It was being reported in France by June 1496, moreover, that King Charles intended to have a papal election in France and to have Cardinal della Rovere elected pope.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 1497 Pope Alexander deprived Cardinal della Rovere of his benefices as an enemy of the Apostolic See, and Giovanni della Rovere of the Prefecture of Rome. His action against the Cardinal was done not only without the consent of the cardinals in consistory, but in fact over their vigorous objections.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> By June, however, the Pope was in negotiations with the Cardinal for reconciliation and return to Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902V,_p._502_note_*_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902V,_p._502_note_*-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> His benefices were restored to him after an apparent reconciliation with the Pope in August 1498.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902VI,_p._61_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902VI,_p._61-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Louis_XII_and_his_Italian_War">Louis XII and his Italian War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Louis XII and his Italian War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Italian_War_of_1499%E2%80%931504" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian War of 1499–1504">Italian War of 1499–1504</a></div>
<p>King Charles VIII of France, the last of the senior branch of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Valois" title="House of Valois">House of Valois</a>, died on 7 April 1498 after accidentally striking his head on the lintel of a door at the Château d'Amboise. When Cesare Borgia passed through southern France in October 1498 on his way to meet King <a href="/wiki/Louis_XII_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XII of France">Louis XII</a> for his investiture as <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Valentinois" title="Duke of Valentinois">Duke of Valentinois</a>, he stopped in Avignon and was magnificently entertained by Cardinal della Rovere.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> They then moved on to meet the King at <a href="/wiki/Chinon" title="Chinon">Chinon</a>, where Cesare Borgia fulfilled one of the terms of the treaty between Louis and Alexander by producing the red hat of a cardinal, which had been promised for the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Rouen" title="Roman Catholic Archdiocese of Rouen">Archbishop of Rouen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Georges_d%27Amboise" title="Georges d&#39;Amboise">Georges d'Amboise</a>. It was Cardinal della Rovere, the Papal Legate, who placed the hat on Amboise's head.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.2,_p._444_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregorovius1900VII.2,_p._444-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Louis wanted an annulment from <a href="/wiki/Joan_of_France,_Duchess_of_Berry" title="Joan of France, Duchess of Berry">Queen Joan</a> so he could marry <a href="/wiki/Anne_of_Brittany" title="Anne of Brittany">Anne of Brittany</a>, in the hope of annexing the Duchy of Brittany; Alexander, in turn, wanted a French princess as wife for Cesare. Della Rovere, who was trying to repair his relations with the House of Borgia, was also involved in another clause of the treaty, the marriage between Cesare Borgia and <a href="/wiki/Charlotte_of_Naples" title="Charlotte of Naples">Carlotta, the daughter of the King of Naples</a>, who had been brought up at the French Court. Della Rovere was in favor of the marriage, but, according to Pope Alexander, King Louis XII was not, and, most especially, Carlotta was stubbornly refusing her consent. Alexander's plan of securing a royal throne for his son fell through, and he was very angry.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Louis offered Cesare another of his relatives, the "beautiful and rich" <a href="/wiki/Charlotte_d%27Albret" class="mw-redirect" title="Charlotte d&#39;Albret">Charlotte d'Albret</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> whom Cesare married at Blois on 13 May 1499.
</p><p>The marriage produced a complete <i>volta facie</i> in Pope Alexander. He became an open partisan of the French and Venice, and accepted their goal, the destruction of the Sforza hold on Milan. On 14 July, Cardinal Ascanio Sforza, della Rovere's sworn enemy, fled Rome with all his property and friends.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._54_n._613_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEubel1914p._54_n._613-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, the French army crossed the Alps and captured Alessandria in Piedmont. On 1 September 1499 Lodovico <i>Il Moro</i> fled Milan, and on 6 September the city surrendered to the French. Cardinal Giuliano was in the King's entourage when he entered Milan on 6 October.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902VI,_pp._68–71_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902VI,_pp._68–71-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Alexander then turned his attention, stimulated by the Venetians, to the threat of the Osmanli Turks. In the autumn of 1499, he called for a crusade and sought aid and money from all Christendom. The rulers of Europe paid little attention, but to show his sincerity Alexander imposed a tithe on all the residents of the Papal States and a tithe on the clergy of the entire world. A list of cardinals and their incomes, drawn up for the occasion, shows that Cardinal della Rovere was the second-richest cardinal, with an annual income of 20,000 <a href="/wiki/Ducat" title="Ducat">ducats</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Another break in relations between Pope Alexander and Cardinal Giuliano came at the end of 1501 or the beginning of 1502 when Giuliano was transferred from the Bishopric of Bologna to the diocese of Vercelli.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 21 June 1502, Pope Alexander sent his secretary, Francesco Troche (Trochia), and Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Amanieu_d%27Albret" title="Amanieu d&#39;Albret">Amanieu d'Albret</a> (brother-in-law of Cesare Borgia) to <a href="/wiki/Savona" title="Savona">Savona</a> to seize Cardinal della Rovere by stealth and bring him back to Rome as quickly as possible and turn him over to the Pope. The kidnapping party returned to Rome on 12 July, without having accomplished its mission.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> On 20 July 1502, Cardinal Giovanni Battista Ferrari died in his rooms at the Vatican Palace; he had been poisoned, and his property was claimed by the Borgia.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> On 3 January 1503, <a href="/wiki/Giambattista_Orsini" title="Giambattista Orsini">Cardinal Orsini</a> was arrested and sent to the Castel Sant'Angelo; on 22 February he died there, poisoned on the orders of Alexander VI.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Election">Election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_October_1503" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, October 1503">Papal conclave, October 1503</a></div>
<p>A veteran of the Sacred College, della Rovere had won influence for the election of Pope Pius III with the help of Florentine Ambassador to Naples, Lorenzo de' Medici. In spite of a violent temper della Rovere succeeded by dexterous diplomacy in winning the support of Cesare Borgia, whom he won over by his promise of money and continued papal backing for Borgia policies in the Romagna.<sup id="cite_ref-Britannica_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Britannica-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> This election was, in Ludwig von Pastor's view, certainly achieved by means of bribery with money, but also with promises. "Giuliano, whom the popular voice seemed to indicate as the only possible pope, was as unscrupulous as any of his colleagues in the means which he employed. Where promises and persuasions were unavailing, he did not hesitate to have recourse to bribery."<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Indeed, his election on 1 November 1503 took only a few hours, and the only two votes he did not receive were his own and the one of <a href="/wiki/Georges_d%27Amboise" title="Georges d&#39;Amboise">Georges d'Amboise</a>, his most vigorous opponent and the favourite of the <a href="/wiki/French_monarchy" class="mw-redirect" title="French monarchy">French monarchy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> In the end, as in all papal elections, the vote is made unanimous after the leading candidate has achieved the required number of votes for election.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="A_Renaissance_pope">A Renaissance pope</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: A Renaissance pope">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pier_maria_serbaldi_da_pescia,_medaglia_di_giulio_II,_recto_(oro).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Pier_maria_serbaldi_da_pescia%2C_medaglia_di_giulio_II%2C_recto_%28oro%29.JPG/220px-Pier_maria_serbaldi_da_pescia%2C_medaglia_di_giulio_II%2C_recto_%28oro%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Pier_maria_serbaldi_da_pescia%2C_medaglia_di_giulio_II%2C_recto_%28oro%29.JPG/330px-Pier_maria_serbaldi_da_pescia%2C_medaglia_di_giulio_II%2C_recto_%28oro%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Pier_maria_serbaldi_da_pescia%2C_medaglia_di_giulio_II%2C_recto_%28oro%29.JPG/440px-Pier_maria_serbaldi_da_pescia%2C_medaglia_di_giulio_II%2C_recto_%28oro%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1260" data-file-height="1260" /></a><figcaption>Medal in gold, by Pier Maria Serbaldi da Pescia</figcaption></figure><p>
Giuliano Della Rovere took the name Julius, only used by a single fourth-century predecessor, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Julius_I" title="Pope Julius I">Julius I</a>, and was pope for nine years, from 1503 to 1513. From the beginning, Julius II set out to defeat the various powers that challenged his temporal authority; in a series of complicated stratagems, he first succeeded in rendering it impossible for the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Borgia" title="House of Borgia">Borgias</a> to retain their power over the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>. Indeed, on the day of his election, he issued a <i><a href="/wiki/Damnatio_memoriae" title="Damnatio memoriae">damnatio memoriae</a></i>, declaring:<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>
I will not live in the same rooms as the Borgias lived. He [Alexander VI] desecrated the Holy Church as none before. He usurped the papal power by the devil's aid, and I forbid under the pain of excommunication anyone to speak or think of Borgia again. His name and memory must be forgotten. It must be crossed out of every document and memorial. His reign must be obliterated. All paintings made of the Borgias or for them must be covered over with black crepe. All the tombs of the Borgias must be opened and their bodies sent back to where they belong – to Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-NC2_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NC2-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote><p> Others indicate that his decision was taken on 26 November 1507, not in 1503.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The Borgia Apartments were turned to other uses. The <i>Sala de Papi</i> was redecorated by two pupils of Raphael by order of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_X" title="Pope Leo X">Pope Leo X</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902VI,_p._173_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902VI,_p._173-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> The rooms were used to accommodate Emperor Charles V on his visit to the Vatican after the <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Rome_(1527)" title="Sack of Rome (1527)">Sack of Rome (1527)</a>, and subsequently, they became the residence of the <a href="/wiki/Cardinal-nephew" title="Cardinal-nephew">Cardinal-nephew</a> and then the Secretary of State.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julius used his influence to reconcile two powerful Roman families, the <a href="/wiki/Orsini_family" title="Orsini family">Orsini</a> and <a href="/wiki/Colonna_family" title="Colonna family">Colonna</a>. Decrees were made in the interests of the Roman nobility, in whose shoes the new pope now stepped. Being thus secure in Rome and the surrounding country, he set himself the task to expel the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Republic of Venice</a> from <a href="/wiki/Faenza" title="Faenza">Faenza</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rimini" title="Rimini">Rimini</a>, and the other towns and fortresses of Italy which it occupied after the death of Pope Alexander.<sup id="cite_ref-Ott1910_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ott1910-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Shaw_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shaw-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> In 1504, finding it impossible to succeed with the <a href="/wiki/Doge_of_Venice" title="Doge of Venice">Doge of Venice</a> by remonstrance, he brought about a union of the conflicting interests of <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Holy Roman Empire</a>, and sacrificed temporarily to some extent the independence of Italy to conclude with them an offensive and defensive alliance against <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Norwich1989_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Norwich1989-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The combination was, however, at first little more than nominal, and was not immediately effective in compelling the Venetians to deliver up more than a few unimportant places in the <a href="/wiki/Romagna" title="Romagna">Romagna</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Ott1910_53-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ott1910-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> With a campaign in 1506, he personally led an army to <a href="/wiki/Perugia" title="Perugia">Perugia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a>, freeing the two papal cities from their despots, Giampolo Baglioni and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_II_Bentivoglio" title="Giovanni II Bentivoglio">Giovanni II Bentivoglio</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Ott1910_53-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ott1910-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 1503, Julius issued a dispensation allowing the future <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry VIII of England">Henry VIII of England</a> to marry <a href="/wiki/Catherine_of_Aragon" title="Catherine of Aragon">Catherine of Aragon</a>; Catherine had previously been briefly married to Henry's older brother <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Tudor" class="mw-redirect" title="Arthur Tudor">Prince Arthur</a>, who had died, but Henry later argued that she had remained a virgin for the five months of the marriage. Some twenty years later, when Henry was attempting to wed <a href="/wiki/Anne_Boleyn" title="Anne Boleyn">Anne Boleyn</a> (since his son by Catherine of Aragon survived only a few days, and two of her sons were stillborn, and therefore he had no male heir), he sought to have his marriage annulled, claiming that the dispensation of Pope Julius should never have been issued. The retraction of the dispensation was refused by Pope <a href="/wiki/Clement_VII" class="mw-redirect" title="Clement VII">Clement VII</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:History_of_the_great_reformation_in_Europe_in_the_times_of_Luther_and_Calvin.._(1870)_(14785634573).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/History_of_the_great_reformation_in_Europe_in_the_times_of_Luther_and_Calvin.._%281870%29_%2814785634573%29.jpg/220px-History_of_the_great_reformation_in_Europe_in_the_times_of_Luther_and_Calvin.._%281870%29_%2814785634573%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="134" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/History_of_the_great_reformation_in_Europe_in_the_times_of_Luther_and_Calvin.._%281870%29_%2814785634573%29.jpg/330px-History_of_the_great_reformation_in_Europe_in_the_times_of_Luther_and_Calvin.._%281870%29_%2814785634573%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/History_of_the_great_reformation_in_Europe_in_the_times_of_Luther_and_Calvin.._%281870%29_%2814785634573%29.jpg/440px-History_of_the_great_reformation_in_Europe_in_the_times_of_Luther_and_Calvin.._%281870%29_%2814785634573%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3696" data-file-height="2248" /></a><figcaption>Jetzer being tricked. Jetzer was a Dominican friar in Bern, and some of his brothers tricked him into thinking he was receiving a revelation from the Virgin Mary. Eventually he figured it out. In punishment over this scandal, four Dominicans were burned at the stake under the orders of Pope Julius II with an audience of 30,000 people on 1 May 1509.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>The Bull entitled <i>Ea quae pro bono pacis</i>, issued on 24 January 1506, confirmed papal approval of the <i><a href="/wiki/Mare_clausum" title="Mare clausum">mare clausum</a></i> policy being pursued by <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spain</a> and <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portugal</a> amid their <a href="/wiki/Age_of_Discovery" title="Age of Discovery">explorations</a>, and approved the changes of the 1494 <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Tordesillas" title="Treaty of Tordesillas">Treaty of Tordesillas</a> to previous papal bulls. In the same year, the Pope founded the <a href="/wiki/Swiss_Guard" title="Swiss Guard">Swiss Guard</a> to provide a constant corps of soldiers to protect the pope. As part of the Renaissance program of reestablishing the glory of antiquity for the Christian capital, Rome, Julius II took considerable effort to present himself as a sort of emperor-pope, capable of leading a Latin-Christian empire. On Palm Sunday, 1507, "Julius II entered Rome ... both as a second <a href="/wiki/Julius_Caesar" title="Julius Caesar">Julius Caesar</a>, heir to the majesty of Rome's imperial glory, and in the likeness of Christ, whose vicar the pope was, and who in that capacity governed the universal Roman Church."<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (October 2020)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Julius, who modeled himself after his namesake Caesar, would personally lead his army across the Italian peninsula under the imperial war-cry, "Drive out the barbarians." Yet, despite the imperial rhetoric, the campaigns were highly localized.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Perugia voluntarily surrendered in March 1507 to direct control, as it had always been within the Papal States; it was in these endeavors he had enlisted French mercenaries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlbury2011324_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlbury2011324-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Urbino's magnificent court palace was infiltrated by French soldiers in the pay of the Duke of Gonzaga; the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Montefeltro_Conspiracy&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Montefeltro Conspiracy (page does not exist)">Montefeltro Conspiracy</a> against his loyal cousins earned the occupying armies the Pope's undying hatred.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Julius relied upon Guidobaldo's help to raise his nephew and heir <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Maria_I_della_Rovere,_Duke_of_Urbino" title="Francesco Maria I della Rovere, Duke of Urbino">Francesco Maria della Rovere</a>; the intricate web of nepotism helped secure the Italian Papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlbury2011329_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlbury2011329-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Moreover, the Pope's interest in Urbino was widely known in the French court.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Julius left a spy at the Urbino Palace, possibly <a href="/wiki/Galeotto_Franciotti_della_Rovere" title="Galeotto Franciotti della Rovere">Galeotto Franciotti della Rovere</a>, Cardinal of San Pietro, to watch the Mantua stables in total secret; the secular progress of the Papal Curia was growing in authority and significance. In Rome, the Pope watched from his private chapel to see how his court behaved. This was an age of Renaissance conspiracy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlbury201436–37_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlbury201436–37-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="League_of_Cambrai_and_Holy_League">League of Cambrai and Holy League</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: League of Cambrai and Holy League">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Leonardo_della_Rovere.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/d/d8/Leonardo_della_Rovere.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="267" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="200" data-file-height="267" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Leonardo_Grosso_della_Rovere" title="Leonardo Grosso della Rovere">Leonardo Grosso della Rovere</a>, the fourth <a href="/wiki/Cardinal-nephew" title="Cardinal-nephew">Cardinal-nephew</a> of Julius II, accompanied him on his military campaigns in <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a> and <a href="/wiki/Perugia" title="Perugia">Perugia</a>, and served as his ambassador to France.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In addition to an active military policy, the new pope personally led troops into battle on at least two occasions, the first to expel Giovanni Bentivoglio from Bologna (17 August 1506 – 23 March 1507), which was achieved successfully with the assistance of the Duchy of Urbino. The second was an attempt to recover Ferrara for the Papal States (1 September 1510 – 29 June 1512).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrassi1886_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrassi1886-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (March 2021)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> In 1508, Julius was fortuitously able to form the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Cambrai" class="mw-redirect" title="League of Cambrai">League of Cambrai</a> with <a href="/wiki/Louis_XII_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XII of France">Louis XII, King of France</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor">Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor</a> (proclaimed without coronation as Emperor by Pope Julius II at Trent in 1508) and <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon" title="Ferdinand II of Aragon">Ferdinand II, King of Aragon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> The League fought against the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Republic of Venice</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> Among other things, Julius wanted possession of Venetian <a href="/wiki/Romagna" title="Romagna">Romagna</a>; Emperor Maximilian I wanted <a href="/wiki/Friuli" title="Friuli">Friuli</a> and <a href="/wiki/Veneto" title="Veneto">Veneto</a>; Louis XII wanted Cremona, and Ferdinand II desired the <a href="/wiki/Apulia" title="Apulia">Apulian</a> ports.<sup id="cite_ref-Shaw_79-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shaw-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> This war was a conflict in what was collectively known as the "<a href="/wiki/Italian_Wars" title="Italian Wars">Italian Wars</a>". In the spring of 1509, the Republic of Venice was placed under an <a href="/wiki/Interdict" title="Interdict">interdict</a> by Julius,<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> In May 1509 Julius sent troops to fight against the Venetians who had occupied parts of the <a href="/wiki/Romagna" title="Romagna">Romagna</a>, winning back the Papal States in a decisive battle near Cremona.<sup id="cite_ref-Ott1910_53-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ott1910-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>During the War of the Holy League, alliances kept changing: in 1510 Venice and France switched places, and by 1513, Venice had joined France.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> The achievements of the League soon outstripped the primary intention of Julius. In one single battle, the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Agnadello" title="Battle of Agnadello">Battle of Agnadello</a> on 14 May 1509, the dominion of Venice in Italy was practically lost to the pope. Neither the King of France nor the Holy Roman Emperor was satisfied with merely effecting the purposes of the Pope; the latter found it necessary to enter into an arrangement with the Venetians to defend himself from those who immediately before had been his allies.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> The Venetians, on making humble submission, were absolved at the beginning of 1510, and shortly afterward France was placed under papal <a href="/wiki/Interdict" title="Interdict">interdict</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sisto_della_Rovere.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/6/62/Sisto_della_Rovere.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="267" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="200" data-file-height="267" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Sisto_Gara_della_Rovere" title="Sisto Gara della Rovere">Sisto Gara della Rovere</a>, the fifth cardinal nephew of Julius II, was the Prior in Rome of the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Hospitaller" title="Knights Hospitaller">Knights Hospitaller of Malta</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Attempts to cause a rupture between France and <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a> proved unsuccessful; on the other hand, at a <a href="/wiki/Synod" title="Synod">synod</a> convened by Louis at <a href="/wiki/Tours" title="Tours">Tours</a> in September 1510, the French bishops withdrew from papal obedience and resolved, with the Emperor's co-operation, to seek dethronement of the pope. With some courage Julius marched his army to Bologna and then against the French to <a href="/wiki/Mirandola" title="Mirandola">Mirandola</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Ott1910_53-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ott1910-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> In November 1511, a council met at Pisa, called by rebel cardinals with support from the French king and the Empire; they demanded the deposition of Julius II at <a href="/wiki/Pisa" title="Pisa">Pisa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> He refused to shave, showing utter contempt for the hated French occupation. "per vendicarsi et diceva ... anco fuora scazato el re Ludovico Franza d'Italia."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902VI,_p.339_n_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902VI,_p.339_n-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:La_Resa_di_Mirandola_(1810).JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/La_Resa_di_Mirandola_%281810%29.JPG/220px-La_Resa_di_Mirandola_%281810%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="127" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/La_Resa_di_Mirandola_%281810%29.JPG/330px-La_Resa_di_Mirandola_%281810%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/La_Resa_di_Mirandola_%281810%29.JPG/440px-La_Resa_di_Mirandola_%281810%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1177" data-file-height="679" /></a><figcaption><i>Pope Julius II on the walls of the <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Mirandola_(1511)" title="Siege of Mirandola (1511)">conquered</a> city of <a href="/wiki/Mirandola" title="Mirandola">Mirandola</a></i> (oil on canvas by <a href="/wiki/Raffaello_Tancredi" title="Raffaello Tancredi">Raffaello Tancredi</a>, 1890, City Hall of <a href="/wiki/Mirandola" title="Mirandola">Mirandola</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Whereupon Julius entered into another <a href="/wiki/Holy_League_(1511)" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy League (1511)">Holy League of 1511</a>: in alliance with Ferdinand II of Aragon and the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Venetians</a> he conspired against the Gallican liberties. In a short time, both <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry VIII of England">Henry VIII, King of England</a> (1509–47), and <a href="/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor">Maximilian I</a> also joined the Holy League of 1511 against France. Ferdinand of Spain now recognized Naples as a papal fief, invested in 1511, and therefore Julius II now regarded France as the main foreign power in the Italian peninsula hostile to Papal interests. Louis XII defeated the alliance at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Ravenna_(1512)" title="Battle of Ravenna (1512)">Battle of Ravenna</a> on 11 April 1512. When a desperate battle felled over 20,000 men in a bloodbath the Pope commanded his protege, a newly released young Cardinal Medici to re-take Florence with a Spanish army. The rescue of the city on 1 September 1512 saved Rome from another invasion, ousting <a href="/wiki/Piero_Soderini" title="Piero Soderini">Piero Soderini</a>, and returning the dynastic rule of the Medici. Julius had seemingly restored <i><a href="/wiki/Fortuna" title="Fortuna">fortuna</a></i> or control by exercising his manly <i>vertu</i>, just as Machiavelli wrote. This re-asserted a strong relation between Florence and Rome, a lasting legacy of Julius II. Yet Machiavelli and his methods would not outlast Julius' Papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrathern2003264–266_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrathern2003264–266-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Julius <a href="/wiki/Holy_League_(1511)" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy League (1511)">hired Swiss mercenaries</a> to fight against the French in Milan in May 1512,<sup id="cite_ref-Norwich1989_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Norwich1989-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> causing the French army to withdraw across the Alps into Savoy. The papacy gained control of <a href="/wiki/Parma" title="Parma">Parma</a> and <a href="/wiki/Piacenza" title="Piacenza">Piacenza</a> in central Italy. With the French out of Italy and Spain recognizing Naples as a papal fief, a Congress was held in Mantua by Julius II to declare the liberation of the peninsula. Nevertheless, although Julius had centralized and expanded the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>, he was far from realizing his dream of an independent Italian kingdom. Italy was not at peace either. The French were preparing new campaigns to reconquer Milan, and Julius II confessed to a Venetian ambassador a plan to invest his counselor <a href="/wiki/Luigi_d%27Aragona" title="Luigi d&#39;Aragona">Luigi d'Aragona</a> with the kingdom of Naples in order to end Spanish presence in the south. In fact, after the death of Julius, war would resume and the treaties of Noyon and Brussels in 1516 would again divide much of Italy between French and Spanish influence.
</p>
