<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Lateran_Council">Lateran Council</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Lateran Council">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Fifth_Council_of_the_Lateran" title="Fifth Council of the Lateran">Fifth Council of the Lateran</a></div>
<p>In May 1512 a general or ecumenical council, the <a href="/wiki/Fifth_Council_of_the_Lateran" title="Fifth Council of the Lateran">Fifth Council of the Lateran</a>, was held in Rome. According to an oath taken on his election to observe the <a href="/wiki/Electoral_capitulation" title="Electoral capitulation">Electoral Capitulations</a> of the Conclave of October 1503,<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Julius had sworn to summon a general council, but it had been delayed, he affirmed, because of the occupation of Italy by his enemies.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The real stimulus came from a false council which took place in 1511, later called the <i>Conciliabulum Pisanum</i>, inspired by <a href="/wiki/Louis_XII_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XII of France">Louis XII</a> and <a href="/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor">Maximilian I</a> as a tactic to weaken Julius, threatening to depose him.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Julius' reply was the issuing of the bull <i>Non-sini gravi</i> of 18 July 1511, which fixed the date of 19 April 1512 for the opening of his own council.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> The Council actually convened on 3 May 1512, and <a href="/wiki/Paris_de_Grassis" title="Paris de Grassis">Paris de Grassis</a> reports that the crowd at the basilica was estimated at 50,000.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDöllinger1882417_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDöllinger1882417-107">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> It held its first working session on 10 May.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> In the third plenary session, on 3 December 1512, Julius attended, though ill; but he wanted to witness and receive the formal adhesion of Emperor Maximilian to the Lateran Council and his repudiation of the <i>Conciliabulum Pisanum</i>. This was one of Julius' great triumphs. The Pope was again in attendance at the fourth session on 10 December, this time to hear the accrediting of the Venetian Ambassador as the Serene Republic's representative at the council; he then had the letter of King Louis XI (of 27 November 1461), in which he announced the revocation of the Pragmatic Sanction, read out to the assembly, and demanded that all persons who accepted the Pragmatic Sanction appear before the Council within sixty days to justify their conduct. This was directed against the current French King Louis XII.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The fifth session was held on 16 February, but Pope Julius was too ill to attend. Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Raffaele_Riario" title="Raffaele Riario">Raffaele Riario</a>, the Dean of the College of Cardinals and Bishop of Ostia, presided. The <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Como" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Como">Bishop of Como</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scaramuccia_Trivulzio" title="Scaramuccia Trivulzio">Scaramuccia Trivulzio</a>, then read from the pulpit a papal bull, <i>Si summus rerum</i>, dated that very day and containing within its text the complete bull of 14 January 1505, <i>Cum tam divino</i>. The bull was submitted to the Council fathers for their consideration and ratification. Julius wanted to remind everyone of his legislation on papal conclaves, in particular against simony, and to fix his regulations firmly in canon law so that they could not be dispensed or ignored. Julius was fully aware that his death was imminent, and wished to establish a major reform in his final days. Though he had been a witness to a good deal of simony at papal conclaves and had been a practitioner himself, he was determined to stamp out this abuse.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> The reading of the bull <i>Cum tam divino</i> became a regular feature of the first day of every conclave.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:%27Moses%27_by_Michelangelo_JBU010.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/%27Moses%27_by_Michelangelo_JBU010.jpg/200px-%27Moses%27_by_Michelangelo_JBU010.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="300" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/%27Moses%27_by_Michelangelo_JBU010.jpg/300px-%27Moses%27_by_Michelangelo_JBU010.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/%27Moses%27_by_Michelangelo_JBU010.jpg/400px-%27Moses%27_by_Michelangelo_JBU010.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2520" data-file-height="3776" /></a><figcaption>The monument of Julius II, with Michelangelo's statues of <i><a href="/wiki/Moses_(Michelangelo)" title="Moses (Michelangelo)">Moses</a></i>, with <a href="/wiki/Rachel" title="Rachel">Rachel</a> and <a href="/wiki/Leah" title="Leah">Leah</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>On the Vigil of Pentecost in May 1512, Pope Julius, aware that he was seriously ill and that his health was failing, despite comments on the part of some cardinals about how well he looked, remarked to Paris de Grassis, "They are flattering me; I know better; my strength diminishes from day to day and I cannot live much longer. Therefore I beg you not to expect me at Vespers or at Mass from henceforth."<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, he continued his restless activities, including Masses, visits to churches, and audiences. On the morning of 24 June Paris found the Pope to be very weak.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDöllinger1882420_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDöllinger1882420-112">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> On Christmas Eve, Julius ordered Paris to summon the College of Cardinals and the Sacristan of the Apostolic Palace, since he was so ill that he did not expect to be able to stay alive very long.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> From then until 6 January he was confined to bed, and most of the time with a fever; he had lost his appetite, but the doctors were unable to diagnose his languor. On 4 February he had an extensive conversation with Paris concerning the arrangements for his funeral.
</p><p>Pope Julius was reported to be seriously ill in a dispatch received in Venice on 10 February 1513.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanuto_&#39;&#39;I_Diarii&#39;&#39;Vol._XV_pp._559,_554:_&#39;&#39;Ha_febre_dopia_terzana&#39;&#39;_(malaria)_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanuto_&#39;&#39;I_Diarii&#39;&#39;Vol._XV_pp._559,_554:_&#39;&#39;Ha_febre_dopia_terzana&#39;&#39;_(malaria)-114">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> He received Holy Communion and was granted the plenary indulgence on the morning of 19 February, according to the Venetian Ambassador. On the 20th, according to Paris de Grassis, he received Holy Communion from the hands of Cardinal Raffaele Riario, the Camerlengo. He died of a fever in the night of 20–21 February 1513.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the evening of 21 February, Paris de Grassis conducted the funeral of Julius II, even though the Canons of the Vatican Basilica and the <i>beneficiati</i> refused to cooperate. The body was placed for a time at the Altar of Saint Andrew in the Basilica and was then carried by the Imperial Ambassador, the papal Datary, and two of Paris' assistants to the altar of the Chapel of Pope Sixtus, where the Vicar of the Vatican Basilica performed the final absolution. At the third hour of the evening, the body was laid in a sepulcher between the altar and the wall of the tribune.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDöllinger1882432–433_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDöllinger1882432–433-116">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite the fact that the so-called "<a href="/wiki/Tomb_of_Pope_Julius_II" title="Tomb of Pope Julius II">Tomb of Julius</a>" by <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a> is in <a href="/wiki/San_Pietro_in_Vincoli" title="San Pietro in Vincoli">San Pietro in Vincoli</a> in Rome, Julius is in fact buried in the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_City" title="Vatican City">Vatican</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Michelangelo's tomb was not completed until 1545 and represents a much-abbreviated version of the planned original, which was initially intended for the new <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a>. His remains lay alongside his uncle, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_IV" title="Pope Sixtus IV">Pope Sixtus IV</a>, but were later desecrated during the <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Rome_(1527)" title="Sack of Rome (1527)">Sack of Rome</a> in 1527. Today both men lie in <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a> on the floor in front of the monument to <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_X" title="Pope Clement X">Pope Clement X</a>. A simple marble tombstone marks the site. Julius II was succeeded by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_X" title="Pope Leo X">Pope Leo X</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Bras%C3%A3o_do_Papa_J%C3%BAlio_II_na_Capela_Sistina.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/Bras%C3%A3o_do_Papa_J%C3%BAlio_II_na_Capela_Sistina.jpg/200px-Bras%C3%A3o_do_Papa_J%C3%BAlio_II_na_Capela_Sistina.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="252" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/74/Bras%C3%A3o_do_Papa_J%C3%BAlio_II_na_Capela_Sistina.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="213" data-file-height="268" /></a><figcaption>Coat of arms of Julius II in the Sistine Chapel</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patronage_of_the_arts">Patronage of the arts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Patronage of the arts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Art_patronage_of_Julius_II" title="Art patronage of Julius II">Art patronage of Julius II</a></div>
<p>In 1484 Cardinal Giuliano della Rovere had begun negotiations to persuade <a href="/wiki/Francesco_II_Gonzaga,_Marquess_of_Mantua" title="Francesco II Gonzaga, Marquess of Mantua">Marquis Francesco Gonzaga</a> of Mantua to allow <a href="/wiki/Andrea_Mantegna" title="Andrea Mantegna">Andrea Mantegna</a> to come to Rome, which finally bore fruit in 1488; Mantegna was given the commission to decorate the chapel of the Belvedere for <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_VIII" title="Pope Innocent VIII">Pope Innocent VIII</a>, on which he spent two years.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Beyond Julius II's political and military achievements, he enjoys a title to honor in his patronage of art, architecture, and literature.<sup id="cite_ref-Baldwin_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Baldwin-119">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> He did much to improve and beautify the city.
</p><p>Early in his papacy, Julius decided to revive the plan for replacing the dilapidated Constantinian basilica of St. Peter's. The idea was not his, but originally that of <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_V" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicholas V">Nicholas V</a>, who had commissioned designs from Bernardo Rossellino. Other more pressing problems distracted the attention of Nicholas and subsequent popes, but Julius was not the sort of person to be distracted once he had settled on an idea, in this case, for the greatest building on earth, for the glory of Saint Peter and himself. In the competition for a building plan, the design of Rossellino was immediately rejected as being out of date. A second design was submitted by Giuliano da Sangallo, an old friend of Julius, who had worked on several projects for him before, including the palazzo at S. Pietro in Vincoli, and who had left Rome with Julius when he fled the wrath of Alexander VI in 1495. Through Cardinal della Rovere, Sangallo had presented Charles VIII a plan for a palace, and in 1496 he had made a tour of the architectural monuments of Provence,<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> returning to his native Florence in 1497.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> His proposals for S. Peter's, however, were not accepted despite what he believed to be a promise, and he retired in anger to Florence.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 18 April 1506 Pope Julius II laid the foundation stone of the new St. Peter's Basilica for the successful architect, Donato Bramante. However, he also began the demolition of the <a href="/wiki/Old_St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="Old St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">old St. Peter's Basilica</a>, which had stood for more than 1,100 years. He was a friend and patron of <a href="/wiki/Donato_Bramante" title="Donato Bramante">Bramante</a> and <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, and a patron of <a href="/wiki/Michelangelo" title="Michelangelo">Michelangelo</a>. Several of Michelangelo's greatest works (including the painting of the ceiling of the <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Chapel" title="Sistine Chapel">Sistine Chapel</a>) were commissioned by Julius. In the framework of Rome's urban renewal (<i>Renovatio Romae</i>), the pope commissioned to Bramante the creation of two new straight streets respectively on the left and right bank of the Tiber: the <a href="/wiki/Via_Giulia" title="Via Giulia">Via Giulia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Via_della_Lungara" title="Via della Lungara">Via della Lungara</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBruschi1971_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBruschi1971-123">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Character">Character</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Character">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:File_by_Alexander_Baranov_-_%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%89%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%BE%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%82%D0%B0_(9520004635).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/File_by_Alexander_Baranov_-_%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%89%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%BE%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%82%D0%B0_%289520004635%29.jpg/220px-File_by_Alexander_Baranov_-_%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%89%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%BE%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%82%D0%B0_%289520004635%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/File_by_Alexander_Baranov_-_%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%89%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%BE%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%82%D0%B0_%289520004635%29.jpg/330px-File_by_Alexander_Baranov_-_%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%89%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%BE%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%82%D0%B0_%289520004635%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d6/File_by_Alexander_Baranov_-_%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%89%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%BE%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%82%D0%B0_%289520004635%29.jpg/440px-File_by_Alexander_Baranov_-_%D0%97%D0%B0%D1%89%D0%B8%D1%82%D0%B0_%D0%BF%D1%80%D0%BE%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%82%D0%B0_%289520004635%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2882" data-file-height="1921" /></a><figcaption>Julius commissioning works from <a href="/wiki/Bramante" class="mw-redirect" title="Bramante">Bramante</a> and <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Alexander_Baranov_(painter)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Alexander Baranov (painter) (page does not exist)">Alexander Baranov</a>, <a href="/wiki/Louvre" title="Louvre">Louvre</a>, 1827</figcaption></figure>
<p>Long before he became Pope, Julius had a violent temper. He often treated subordinates and people who worked for him very badly. His manner was gruff and coarse, just as his peasant-like sense of humour. Others suggest that Julius had little sense of humor. Ludwig von Pastor wrote, "Paris de Grassis, his Master of Ceremonies, who has handed on to us so many characteristic features of his master's life, says that he hardly ever jested. He was generally absorbed in deep and silent thought."<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To most historians Julius was manly and virile, an energetic man of action, whose courage saved the Papacy.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> There was a sense that war caused him serious illness, exhaustion, and fatigue, that most popes could not have withstood. To many Julius II has been described as the best in an era of exceptionally bad popes: Alexander VI was evil and despotic, exposing the future Julius II to a number of assassination attempts that required tremendous fortitude.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Physical_appearance">Physical appearance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Physical appearance">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .mbox-text-span{margin-left:23px!important}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .ambox{margin:0 10%}}</style><table class="box-Unreferenced_section plainlinks metadata ambox ambox-content ambox-Unreferenced" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/50px-Question_book-new.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="39" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/75px-Question_book-new.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/100px-Question_book-new.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="399" /></a></span></div></td><td class="mbox-text"><div class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>does not <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources">cite</a> any <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">sources</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit">improve this section</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">removed</a>.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">October 2020</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Julius II is usually depicted with a beard, after his appearance in the celebrated <a href="/wiki/Portrait_of_Pope_Julius_II_(Raphael)" class="mw-redirect" title="Portrait of Pope Julius II (Raphael)">portrait</a> by <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a>, the artist whom he first met in 1509. However, the pope only wore his beard from 27 June 1511 to March 1512, as a sign of mourning at the loss of the city of <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>. He was nevertheless the first pope since antiquity to grow facial hair, a practice otherwise forbidden by <a href="/wiki/Canon_law" title="Canon law">canon law</a> since the 13th century. The pope's hirsute chin may have raised severe, even vulgar criticism, as at one Bologna banquet held in 1510 at which papal legate Marco Cornaro was present. In overturning the ban on beards Pope Julius challenged Gregorian conventional wisdom in dangerous times. Julius shaved his beard again before his death, and his immediate successors were clean-shaven; nonetheless <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_VII" title="Pope Clement VII">Pope Clement VII</a> sported a beard when mourning the <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Rome_(1527)" title="Sack of Rome (1527)">sack of Rome</a>. Thenceforward, all popes were bearded until the death of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_XII" title="Pope Innocent XII">Pope Innocent XII</a> in 1700.
</p><p>The frescoes on the ceiling of Stanza d'Eliodoro in the stanze of Raphael depict the traumatic events in 1510–11 when the Papacy regained its freedom. Although Raphael's original was lost, it was thought to relate closely to the personal iconography of Stanza della Segnatura, commissioned by Pope Julius himself. The Lateran Council that formed the Holy League marked a high point in his personal success. Saved by an allegory to the Expulsion of Helidorus, the French gone, Julius collapsed once again in late 1512, very seriously ill once more.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_relationships_and_sexuality">Personal relationships and sexuality</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Personal relationships and sexuality">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Estancia_de_Heliodoro_(Misa_de_Bolsena),_detail.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Estancia_de_Heliodoro_%28Misa_de_Bolsena%29%2C_detail.jpg/200px-Estancia_de_Heliodoro_%28Misa_de_Bolsena%29%2C_detail.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="349" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/24/Estancia_de_Heliodoro_%28Misa_de_Bolsena%29%2C_detail.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="300" data-file-height="524" /></a><figcaption>Julius II's daughter, <a href="/wiki/Felice_della_Rovere" title="Felice della Rovere">Felice della Rovere</a> <i>(in black)</i>, by <a href="/wiki/Raphael" title="Raphael">Raphael</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Mass_at_Bolsena" title="The Mass at Bolsena">The Mass at Bolsena</a></i></figcaption></figure>
<p>Julius was not the first pope to have fathered children before being elevated to high office, and had a daughter born to <a href="/wiki/Lucrezia_Normanni" class="mw-redirect" title="Lucrezia Normanni">Lucrezia Normanni</a> in 1483 – after he had been made a cardinal.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastore2001_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastore2001-127">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Felice_della_Rovere" title="Felice della Rovere">Felice della Rovere</a> survived into adulthood.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> Shortly after Felice was born, Julius arranged for Lucrezia to marry Bernardino de Cupis, Chamberlain to Julius's cousin, Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Girolamo_Basso_della_Rovere" title="Girolamo Basso della Rovere">Girolamo Basso della Rovere</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMurphy2005pp._ii–iii_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurphy2005pp._ii–iii-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite producing an illegitimate daughter (and having at least one mistress), it was suggested that Julius may have had <a href="/wiki/Homosexual" class="mw-redirect" title="Homosexual">homosexual</a> lovers – although it is not possible to establish this claim. His confrontational style inevitably created enemies and <a href="/wiki/Sodomy" title="Sodomy">sodomy</a> was the "common currency of insult and innuendo".<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Such accusations were made to discredit him, but perhaps in so doing his accusers were exploiting a generally "perceived weakness".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAldrichWotherspoon2003233_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAldrichWotherspoon2003233-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> The Venetians, who were implacably opposed to the pope's new military policy, were among the most vociferous opponents; notable among them was the diarist <a href="/wiki/Girolamo_Priuli_(1476%E2%80%931547)" title="Girolamo Priuli (1476–1547)">Girolamo Priuli</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPriuli1938_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPriuli1938-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (March 2018)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> <a href="/wiki/Erasmus" title="Erasmus">Erasmus</a> also implied sexual misconduct in his 1514 dialogue "<i><a href="/wiki/Julius_Excluded_from_Heaven" title="Julius Excluded from Heaven">Julius Excluded from Heaven</a></i>"; a theme picked up in the denunciation made at the <a href="/wiki/Fifth_Council_of_the_Lateran" title="Fifth Council of the Lateran">conciliabulum of Pisa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Criticism was furthermore made of the sinister influence exerted by his advisor, <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Alidosi" title="Francesco Alidosi">Francesco Alidosi</a>, whom Julius had made a cardinal in 1505. However, it is likely that the closeness was down to the fact that he simply knew how to handle him well.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_131-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> This sexual reputation survived Julius, and the accusation continued to be made without reservation by Protestant opponents in their polemics against "papism" and Catholic decadence.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> The French writer <a href="/wiki/Philippe_de_Mornay" title="Philippe de Mornay">Philippe de Mornay</a> (1549–1623) accused all Italians of being sodomites, but added specifically: "This horror is ascribed to good Julius."<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Depiction">Depiction</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Julius_II&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Depiction">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>Julius features prominently in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Prince" title="The Prince">The Prince</a></i> of <a href="/wiki/Niccol%C3%B2_Machiavelli" title="Niccolò Machiavelli">Niccolò Machiavelli</a> (1532), both as an enemy of leading protagonist <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare Borgia</a>, and as an example of an ecclesiastical prince who consolidates authority and wisely follows <i>Fortuna</i>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Barbara_Tuchman" class="mw-redirect" title="Barbara Tuchman">Barbara Tuchman</a>, in her book <i>The March of Folly: From Troy to Vietnam</i>, offers a narrative of Julius II's career.<sup id="cite_ref-Tuchman1984_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tuchman1984-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> Her overall assessment of Julius is strongly negative,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Naming_conventions_(geographic_names)" title="Wikipedia:Naming conventions (geographic names)"><span title="The geographic scope near this tag is ambiguous. (March 2012)">where?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and she attributes the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Protestant Reformation</a> to his and other Renaissance popes' abuses.<sup id="cite_ref-Tuchman1984_137-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tuchman1984-137">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In the 1965 film <i><a href="/wiki/The_Agony_and_the_Ecstasy_(film)" title="The Agony and the Ecstasy (film)">The Agony and the Ecstasy</a></i> about the life of Michelangelo, Julius is portrayed as a soldier-pope (though without facial hair) by <a href="/wiki/Rex_Harrison" title="Rex Harrison">Rex Harrison</a>. The film is a dramatization based upon <a href="/wiki/The_Agony_and_the_Ecstasy_(novel)" title="The Agony and the Ecstasy (novel)">the 1961 book of the same name</a> by <a href="/wiki/Irving_Stone" title="Irving Stone">Irving Stone</a>.</li>
<li>Della Rovere was portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Alfred_Burke" title="Alfred Burke">Alfred Burke</a> in the 1981 BBC series <i><a href="/wiki/The_Borgias_(1981_TV_series)" title="The Borgias (1981 TV series)">The Borgias</a></i>, by <a href="/wiki/Colm_Feore" title="Colm Feore">Colm Feore</a> in <a href="/wiki/Neil_Jordan" title="Neil Jordan">Neil Jordan</a>'s 2011 series <i><a href="/wiki/The_Borgias_(2011_TV_series)" title="The Borgias (2011 TV series)">The Borgias</a></i>, and by <a href="/wiki/Dejan_%C4%8Cuki%C4%87" title="Dejan Čukić">Dejan Čukić</a> in <a href="/wiki/Tom_Fontana" title="Tom Fontana">Tom Fontana</a>'s 2011 series, <i><a href="/wiki/Borgia_(TV_series)" title="Borgia (TV series)">Borgia</a></i>.</li>
<li>On 30 November 2003, Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Angelo_Sodano" title="Angelo Sodano">Angelo Sodano</a>, then <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_the_Holy_See" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State of the Holy See">Secretary of State of the Holy See</a>, presided in a Eucharistic concelebration commemorating the fifth centenary of the election of Pope Julius II in the Cathedral <a href="/wiki/Basilica" title="Basilica">Basilica</a> of <a href="/wiki/Savona" title="Savona">Savona</a>. In his sermon<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> he explained that <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a>, to pay homage to his great predecessor, had sent him (Sodano) as his Legate. Admitting that it is difficult to understand the methods of government of that time, Sodano stressed that the work of the <a href="/wiki/Pope" title="Pope">Bishop of Rome</a> should be seen in its proper context. Praising Julius for entrusting the construction of St. Peter's Basilica in its present form to the genius of Bramante in 1505, he said it is certain that Julius liked to think big and wanted the Church of Rome to shine before the world with a visible beauty too. The Cardinal stated, "How can we fail to think of him when we contemplate the grandeur of St. Peter's Basilica?" and "How can we forget that it was he who created in 1506 the <a href="/wiki/Swiss_Guard" title="Swiss Guard">Swiss Guard</a> Corps, with the characteristic uniform that we still admire today?" The Cardinal called Pope Julius II "a Pope who strove to serve the Church and to sacrifice himself for her until the Lord called him at the age of 72".</li></ul>

<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-13"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-13">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> The brother of Francesco della Rovere, later <a href="/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_IV" title="Pope Sixtus IV">Pope Sixtus IV</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPastor1902231_(IV)_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPastor1902231_(IV)-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-93"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-93">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> Also known as the "War of the League of Cambrai"</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-126"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-126">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> Until the 20th century, a Cardinal did not have to be in major Holy Orders (Bishop, Priest, Deacon&#160;&#8211;&#32;which involved the vow of celibacy), unless he hoped to vote in a papal conclave. Even then, he could be dispensed.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-129"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-129">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Pompeo_Litta_Biumi" title="Pompeo Litta Biumi">Pompeo Litta</a> mistakenly attributed Felice's two daughters, Giulia and Clarice, to him as well.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>

</li>

<li id="cite_note-50"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-50">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFGregorovius1900">Gregorovius 1900</a>, VII.1, p. 338. Cf. <a href="#CITEREFBurchard1884">Burchard 1884</a>, II, p.26. See also the letter of the Florentine Ambassador Filippo Valori of 22 January 1493 (<a href="#CITEREFBurchard1884">Burchard 1884</a>, II, p. 627), which alludes to the Cardinal's motives and situation. He dates the withdrawal to Ostia on 20 December, but this is probably a <i>lapsus calami</i> or <i>lapsus mentis</i> for <i>XXX</i>.</span>
</li>

<li id="cite_note-Ott1910-53"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Ott1910_53-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Ott1910_53-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Ott1910_53-2"><sup><i><b>c</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Ott1910_53-3"><sup><i><b>d</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Ott1910_53-4"><sup><i><b>e</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Ott1910_53-5"><sup><i><b>f</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFOtt1910" class="citation encyclopaedia cs1">Ott, Michael (1910). "Pope Julius II". <i>The Catholic Encyclopedia</i>. Vol.&#160;8. New York: Robert Appleton Co.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=bookitem&amp;rft.atitle=Pope+Julius+II&amp;rft.btitle=The+Catholic+Encyclopedia&amp;rft.place=New+York&amp;rft.pub=Robert+Appleton+Co.&amp;rft.date=1910&amp;rft.aulast=Ott&amp;rft.aufirst=Michael&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3APope+Julius+II" class="Z3988"></span> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.newadvent.org/cathen/08562a.htm">From New Advent website</a></span>

<li id="cite_note-54"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-54">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"Accompanying the young king on his military campaign, [della Rovere] entered Rome along with him, and endeavoured to instigate the convocation of a council to inquire into the conduct of the pope with a view to [deposing him], but Alexander, having gained a friend in Charles VIII's minister <a href="/wiki/Guillaume_Bri%C3%A7onnet_(Cardinal)" class="mw-redirect" title="Guillaume Briçonnet (Cardinal)"> [Guillaume] Briçonnet</a>, Bishop of S. Malo, by the offer of a cardinal's hat, succeeded in counterworking [defeating] the machinations of his enemy [della Rovere], the death of [Pope] Alexander VI in 1503, where his son <a href="/wiki/Cesare_Borgia" title="Cesare Borgia">Cesare Borgia</a> wished to be elevated, fell ill at the same time Della Rovere supported the candidature of Cardinal Piccolomini of <a href="/wiki/Milan" title="Milan">Milan</a>, who was consecrated under the name of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_III" title="Pope Pius III">Pope Pius III</a> on 8 October 1503,…then suffering from an incurable malady, of which he died in little more than a month afterward." BelfordClarke</span>
</li>
